Return freight insurance strategies for the online retailer and insurance
company
Xiaoxiao Li a,b, Jie Gao a,∗, Yiwen Bian c
a School of Management, The State Key Lab for Manufacturing Systems Engineering, Xi’an Jiaotong University, Xi’an, 710049, China
b Department of Management Sciences, City University of Hong Kong, 999077, Hong Kong Special Administrative Region of China
c SHU-UTS SILC (Sydney Institute of Language & Commerce) Business School, Shanghai University, Shanghai, 201899, China
A R T I C L E
I N F O
Keywords:
Return freight insurance
Quality risk
Customer return
Quality perception
E-commerce
A B S T R A C T
In online purchases, customers often exhibit considerable hesitations due to quality uncertainty and return
freight fees. In response, online retailers may buy the return freight insurance (RFI) service from insurance
companies that compensate the customers when they return the purchased item. With the RFI service, insurance
companies generally verify the quality information posted by the retailer to avoid excessive customer returns.
Thus, the RFI service not only reduces the customer’s return freight but also acts as an imperfect quality signal
for customers to mitigate quality uncertainty. We characterize the RFI’s imperfect signaling effect by signal
efficiency, and develop a game-theoretic model to investigate the RFI strategies of insurance companies and
the retailer. Our results shed light on how signal efficiency and return freight fees drive their RFI strategies.
The insurance companies charge an extremely small RFI premium for the retailer selling high-quality products;
for the retailer selling low-quality products, the RFI premium becomes large for those with a medium return
freight fee, or with a small signal efficiency. The retailer selling high-quality products always adopts the RFI
service. The retailer selling low-quality products does not adopt the RFI service when the return freight fee
is large. Given a medium return freight fee, the retailer with low-quality products prefers the RFI service
when the signal efficiency is medium. Given a small return freight fee, only when the signal efficiency is large
does the retailer with low-quality products adopt the RFI service. Our results provide new explanations for
the prevalence of the RFI service for retailers selling products of different quality, and shed light on the RFI
premium strategy for insurance companies.
1. Introduction
Customers often face quality uncertainty risk, i.e., uncertain product performance, when shopping online, as they cannot fully understand the
true product quality, e.g., the warm performance of clothing (Guo and Zhao, 2009; Guan and Chen, 2015) or computer performance, via text
description, pictures and videos (Nelson, 1970; Guo and Zhao, 2009; Board, 2009; Feng et al., 2020; Hsiao and Chen, 2012). To reduce the quality
uncertainty risk, most online retailers provide the money-back-guarantee (MBG) policy. An MBG policy allows customers to return products if they
are not satisfied with the true quality.1 For example, online down coat retailers often claim the coat is padded with duck down and the fabric
quantity is 150 g. However, the warmth of a down coat also depends on many other factors, e.g., fill power and feather content. At the purchase
stage, the customer is not clear about the actual performance of the product and makes purchase decisions based on the perceived quality inferred
from the posted texts and pictures. When the product is delivered, the customer learns the actual performance. If the performance is far below the
perceived quality, the customer feels unsatisfied and chooses to return the product.
However, even if a full-refund policy (e.g., MBG policy) is offered, customers still have to bear the return freight fee, which becomes an inhibitor
for online purchases (Lin et al., 2020). Surveys show that 66% of customers are concerned about return freight fees2 and 79% want free return

freight.3 Moreover, more than 69% of customers opt out of online shopping because they are unwilling to bear the return freight fee.4 Bower and
Maxham (2012) suggest that online retailers should institute a policy of free product return. To this end, the return freight insurance (RFI) service
for online retailers (retailer-oriented RFI) is introduced on platforms such as Taobao in China. Taobao has invited insurance companies to offer
the RFI service for online retailers since 2010. With the release of the ‘‘Antitrust Guidelines for the Platform Economy Industry’’, Taobao focuses
on reducing entry barriers for insurance companies to provide the RFI service.5 For example, more than ten insurance companies, such as PICC
and Huatai Insurance, offer the RFI service on Taobao.6 In recent years, the RFI service has become popular on online platforms such as JD and
Suning.
The retailer on the platform is eligible to decide whether to adopt this service. For example, most make-up and apparel retailers on Taobao
provide the RFI service, whereas some daily necessity retailers do not. If the retailer adopts the RFI service, the insurance companies determine
the unit RFI premium based on the evaluation of online retailers’ quality/return rates, return freight fees, and product category.7 Afterwards, the
retailer pays the premium in pace with each transaction via the online shopping platform to the RFI service provider. Once a customer returns
his/her purchase, the insurance company pays a freight compensation fee to the customer.
The retailer may exaggerate product quality to solicit purchases from customers. In the presence of RFI, the exaggerated quality leads to more
returns and incurs more compensation costs for the RFI provider. Hence, the insurance companies generally evaluate the product quality before
determining the RFI premium8 and are entitled to rescind the contract with high-return-rate retailers, forcing the retailer to post the true quality
as much as possible.7 From the perspective of customer, the RFI service acts as a third-party quality certificate (Zhang et al., 2021), and can be
regarded as a signal of the true quality (Shao et al., 2013; Zhang et al., 2021). Nevertheless, the RFI service is an imperfect quality signal, i.e., the
RFI service cannot indicate the product quality precisely, since this signaling effect may not always work due to the difficulties in describing
the product performance through text and pictures. The efficiency of this signal (in short, signal efficiency) captures the intertemporal difference
between the customer’s perceived and true quality, and is associated with the product category. In particular, for some experience-based products,
such as apparel and make-up, the evaluation of these products’ performance depends heavily on users’ subjective experience (Nelson, 1970; Gupta
et al., 2004; Wells et al., 2011), and the retailer is likely to disclose false quality information without third-party censorship. Hence, customers
rely on the insurance company’s censorship of quality information, implying high signal efficiency for these experience-based products (Kang and
Johnson, 2009). In contrast, for categories like daily necessities, product quality is objective and straightforward for customers to evaluate by text
description. Thus, customers encounter less quality risk and rely less on the insurance company’s censorship of quality information, and thus the
signal is less efficient (Alba and Hutchinson, 1987; Coupey et al., 1998; Söderlund, 2002). This imperfect signaling effect, which is product-category
contingent, distinguishes the RFI service from the extant return policies. Thus, the wisdom of managing return policies may not apply to the RFI
service. However, few current studies consider this imperfect signaling effect; rather, this paper incorporates this effect and highlights its impact
on the retailer’s and insurance companies’ strategies.
As the RFI service makes up for the customers’ return freight fee and encourages customers to return the product, it causes additional costs for
both the retailer and the insurance companies. Hence, it is important for insurance companies to charge a proper RFI premium to cover its cost
and solicit more retailers to adopt the RFI service. Intriguingly, most research focuses on the retailer’s RFI decision by assuming an exogenous RFI
premium. Few attempts in academia consider the RFI premium decision of the insurance companies. Several research questions naturally arise:
(1) What RFI premium should the competitive insurance companies charge for the retailer?
(2) When should the retailer adopt the RFI service?
(3) How should the retailer set the retail price if it adopts the RFI service?
To address these questions, we propose a game-theoretic model consisting of one retailer and several competitive insurance companies who sell
homogeneous RFI services. They serve a customer population with quality risk and heterogeneous willingness-to-pay (WTP). We examine the pricing
strategies of the retailer and competitive insurance companies by considering two possible scenarios: 1) the retailer does not adopt the RFI service,
and 2) the retailer adopts the RFI service. Under each scenario, the customer does not observe the true product quality before the purchase, but can
update the belief on the quality if the RFI service is adopted. The customer then makes the purchase decision based on the perceived quality. After
the transaction, the customer learns the true quality and decides whether to return the purchase. The customer who returns the product obtains
full refund and freight compensation from the insurance company if the retailer adopts the RFI. Otherwise, the customer obtains the full refund
without any freight compensation.
In this study, customers can be categorized into three classes according to their WTP: compulsive customers with high WTP who never return
the product, strategic customers with medium WTP who return the low-quality product but keep the high-quality product, and non-buy customers
with low WTP who never buy the product.
Our results show that the competitive insurance companies set an infinitesimally small RFI premium for the retailer selling high-quality products,
because both the compulsive and strategic customers always keep the high-quality product. In contrast, the retailer selling low-quality products
may encounter customer returns, and thus the RFI premium is closely related to the return rate. This result is distinct from the expected-quality
related premium indicated by Zhang et al. (2021), who assume that the insurance company lacks the quality information on the retailer. In contrast,
we consider a distinct information structure in that the insurance company knows the true quality. Our result further shows that the RFI premium
for the low-quality retailer first increases then decreases in the return freight fee due to the trade-off between the unit compensation cost and the
return rate. In addition, the RFI premium decreases in the signal efficiency. This is because the high signal efficiency effectively prevents strategic
customers from purchasing, reducing the return rate. This result echoes the phenomenon in which the insurance company, AXA, on Suning.com,
charges a smaller premium (0.85 RMB) for make-up with high signal efficiency, compared with products like food (0.95 RMB) and household
appliances (1.00 RMB).9

The retailer’s adoption decision of the RFI service is determined by the trade-off between the sales revenue advantage and the extra RFI costs
including both the premium and return handling costs. The retailer selling high-quality products is always willing to adopt the RFI service as RFI
improves the customers’ purchase utility due to its quality signaling effect, and the retailer bears no return cost; whereby the retailer is able to
raise the price and ends up with a high sales revenue. The RFI adoption decision for the retailer selling low-quality products is somewhat nuanced,
shaped by the synergistic effect of signal efficiency and return freight fee. Specifically, when the return freight fee is large (e.g., bulk merchandise),
the retailer is less likely to adopt the RFI service, consistent with the empirical finding of Chen et al. (2018). Note that for the retailer selling
low-quality products, the large return freight fee only induces purchases of the compulsive customers, yet the quality signaling role of RFI drags
the customers’ perceived quality down, resulting in a low price and a low sales revenue. When the return freight fee is medium, both the compulsive
and strategic customers may purchase the product. The presence of RFI dwindles the strategic customers’ expected utility to the low-quality product,
attributed again to its quality signaling role, and thus effectively prevents strategic customers from purchasing. In this case, the retailer is more
likely to adopt RFI when the signal efficiency is moderate in case of diminishing compulsive customers. When the return freight fee is so small that
the retailer accommodates a proportion of strategic customers, only a high signal efficiency could effectively dissuade the strategic customers from
purchasing the product, thus decreasing the return rate as well as the RFI costs. In this case, the retailer prefers the RFI service when the signal
efficiency is high. These results give explanations of the RFI adoption for retailers selling products with different return freight fees. For example,
both furniture and apparel are experience products, for which the signal efficiency of RFI is high. The furniture retailer is less likely to adopt RFI,
while the apparel retailers are more willing to adopt RFI.
Upon adopting the RFI service, intuitively, the retailer should pull up the price to make up for additional RFI costs, as indicated by Fan and
Chen (2020), Lin et al. (2020). Our study shows a similar result for the retailer selling high-quality products, which is attributed to the beneficial
quality signaling effect of RFI. Intriguingly, with quite small return freight fees, the retailer selling low-quality products may lower the price when
adopting the RFI service. This is because the retailer must accommodate the strategic customers regardless of the RFI adoption, but the volume
of compulsive customers with RFI is smaller than that without RFI. Hence, by adopting RFI, the retailer tends to decrease its price to expand the
volume of compulsive ones and inhibits returns from the strategic ones.
Finally, we consider the case of a monopoly insurance company. The monopoly insurance company has a larger power to set a high RFI premium
and makes positive returns. As a result, the retailer earns less profit, and thus has less incentive to use RFI. This result explains why online platforms
such as Taobao encourage more insurance companies to participate.
In summary, we underscore the RFI’s imperfect signaling effect and examine the RFI strategies of the retailer and insurance companies. Our
results provide new explanations for the popularity of RFI service for retailers with various quality and return freight fees. We also shed light on
the premium decisions of insurance companies.
The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section 2 summarizes the related literature. We describe our model in Section 3. In Section 4,
we examine the pricing and RFI strategies of the retailer and the insurance companies. We extend to consider the case with a single insurance
company in Section 5. Section 6 provides concluding remarks. We relegate the proofs to Appendices A and B.
2. Literature review
Our study is related to two main research streams. The first stream is about customer returns, and the second is about RFI.
Customer returns are often incurred by product quality risk. The extant studies on customer returns characterize the horizontal quality risk,
that a product may not match the customer’s tastes. From the modeling perspective, most assume customers are associated with an exogenous
return probability and examine the impact of product mismatch risk on operational decisions (see Chen and Chen (2017a,b), Ma et al. (2020)).
Anderson et al. (2009) instead consider the endogenous customer return behavior, allowing the firm to measure the value of the return option to
customers. Other studies consider that customer returns originate from the vertical quality risk of satisfaction with product performance, and regard
the quality as the retailer’s private information (see Feng et al., 2020; Moorthy and Srinivasan, 1995). Moorthy and Srinivasan (1995) consider
the quality-associated return probability and show the signaling role of MBG policy. Feng et al. (2020) examine the quality disclosure strategy of
a manufacturer/retailer in the presence of customer returns. Thus, they model the return probability related to the quality disclosure strategy. Our
research falls into the stream that focuses on vertical quality risk, and we mainly focus on the RFI service. In particular, we borrow part of the
model in Anderson et al. (2009) and characterize the endogenous customer return behavior.
Return hassle costs for customers, e.g., the return freight fee and the restocking fee charged by the retailer when merchandise is returned from
the customer, always accompany customer returns and are a critical inhibitor of online purchases. Regarding general return hassle costs, Davis et al.
(1998) and Hsiao and Chen (2014) examine when the retailer adopts the general low-hassle policy for customers. There are extant studies focusing
on the design of the restocking fee to reduce the store return rate (Shulman et al., 2009, 2011), to distinguish high- and low-type customers (Hsiao
and Chen, 2012), to deal with the customer’s anticipated regrets (Zou et al., 2020). However, little research addresses the impact of the return
freight fee even though it naturally accompanies customer returns, and is vital to customer satisfaction and purchase decisions (Cao et al., 2018;
He et al., 2020). In this study, we incorporate the RFI service that reduces the customer’s return freight fee and highlight the impact of return
freight fees on the RFI policies of the retailer and insurance companies. Our results indicate that the return freight fee non-monotonically affects
the retailer’s RFI adoption and the RFI premium.
The RFI service, a replenishment of the return policies, can effectively reduce the quality uncertainty and alleviate the return freight fee’s adverse
effects. The extant research on the RFI service investigates when RFI benefits online retailers. Fan and Chen (2020) find that the RFI service benefits
the retailer when the premium is low and the compensation is large. They argue that a low premium or a considerable compensation alleviates
the negative impact of price markup on demand. Lin et al. (2020) investigate the retailer’s RFI policy by assuming two types of customers with
valuation uncertainty. They show that a large return freight fee reduces customer returns and enables the retailer to benefit from RFI. Chen et al.
(2021) investigate how the reselling and agency selling formats affect the value of RFI for retailers. They highlight the role of the retailer’s unit
return loss and show that the RFI service benefits retailers more under the reselling format. Li et al. (2018) incorporate retailer competition, and
show that only the retailer with a high return rate benefits from RFI due to the freight compensation effect of RFI.
The above studies all assume exogenous insurance premiums. In contrast, Zhang et al. (2021) examine an insurer’s decisions on premium for
the retailer by assuming both the insurance company and customers encounter quality uncertainty risk. The insurance premium under their setting
is related to the expected quality. Similar to Zhang et al. (2021), our research also considers the signaling role of RFI and examines the insurer’s
premium decisions. However, the distinguishing element of our study from theirs is the incorporation of the imperfect signaling effect of RFI,


========== PAGE 4 ==========
International Journal of Production Economics 256 (2023) 108752
4
X. Li et al.
which is captured by signal efficiency. We show that signal efficiency plays an essential role in the retailer’s pricing and RFI adoption strategies.
Furthermore, we consider the case of competitive insurance companies selling homogeneous RFI services, and only the customer is assumed to face
quality risk. We find that the RFI premiums are determined by the retailer’s return rate, and are distinct for the retailers with different quality.
Apart from the retailer-oriented RFI service, customers can also purchase the RFI service from insurance companies, which is called customer-
oriented RFI. Geng et al. (2017) consider that the customer has the option to purchase RFI while the retailer does not have such an option. Several
studies consider both types of RFI. Li et al. (2021a,b) model customers’ endogenous return behavior and find that the retailer grabs higher profits
under both types of RFI, especially by offering retailer-oriented RFI as the retailer captures all customer surplus. Ren et al. (2021) formulate a
binary model with exogenous customer return probability and reach the opposite result: the insurance company should be neutral in providing
both types of RFI, and the online retailer cannot benefit from both types of RFI as the RFI service reduces market share. Zhang et al. (2021) further
show that the customer-oriented RFI cannot make the insurer profitable. As the above literature shows the weakening benefits of customer-oriented
RFI, our study does not incorporate this type of RFI. Another reason why we do not consider the customer-oriented type is that, one of the goals
of our study is to explore the imperfect signaling effect of RFI, where such an effect stems from the quality information verification of the RFI
provider. However, customer-oriented RFI does not have this effect, and its introduction may undermine our main topic results.
To conclude, our study investigates the retailer-oriented RFI service in the presence of vertical quality risk for customers. We underscore the
quality verification role of insurance companies, incorporating the imperfect signaling effect of RFI. We endogenize customer return behaviors rather
than assume an exogenous return probability to explore how RFI affects the demand and return rate. Furthermore, in addition to the retailer, we
also examine the RFI strategies of competitive insurance companies. Our results show that both insurance companies and retailers with dissimilar
quality can potentially benefit from the RFI service. Their RFI strategies change strategically with several vital factors including signal efficiency,
return freight fee and retailer’s quality.
3. Model setting
Consider an online shopping setting where several competitive insurance companies offer homogeneous RFI services for the online retailer,5
and an online retailer sells a product of quality 𝑞to customers who are uncertain about the product quality before purchase. The product quality
𝑞is either high or low, i.e., 𝑞∈{𝑞𝐻, 𝑞𝐿}. Remarkably, the retailer knows the product quality but cannot use prices to signal quality as prices alone
cannot be an effective quality signal (Grossman, 1981; Olshavsky et al., 1985), and this study mainly focuses on the role of the RFI service in
influencing the customer choice. In brief, the retailer knows the product quality and knows that the customers do not (Narayanan and Manchanda,
2009; Narayanan et al., 2005; Guo et al., 2022).
The retailer provides the MBG policy for the customer, decides whether to adopt the RFI service and sets the retail price. Depending on the
retailer’s RFI decision, we consider two cases: ‘‘the benchmark case without RFI’’ and ‘‘the case with RFI service’’, and denote the two cases by
subscripts 𝑁and 𝑅respectively. Hence, the retailer’s RFI adoption decision is represented by the following indicator function:
𝐼𝑅=
{
1, if the retailer adopts RFI,
0, otherwise..
(1)
The retailer sets the price 𝑝𝑅if it adopts the RFI service (i.e., 𝐼𝑅= 1). Otherwise, it sets the price 𝑝𝑁.
3.1. The customer
The market involves a continuum of customers who are heterogeneous in the willingness-to-pay (WTP) 𝛾for the product quality, where
𝛾∼𝑈(0, 1). Each customer has at most one-unit demand, and the market size is normalized to 1. Initially, the customer does not know the
product’s true quality. Let ̄𝑞be the perceived quality, which is a random variable. The customer decides whether to purchase the product based
on the perceived quality. After the purchase, the customer then learns the product’s true quality and decides whether to keep or return it. In
consideration of this return behavior, the customer seeks to maximize the individual expected surplus at the initial purchase stage.
3.1.1. Without the RFI service
We first consider that the retailer does not adopt the RFI service (i.e., 𝐼𝑅= 0). Before the purchase, the customer holds the initial belief that the
product quality will be 𝑞𝐻with probability 1∕2 and be 𝑞𝐿with probability 1∕2. After receiving the product, the customer immediately learns the
product’s true quality 𝑞. If keeping the product, the customer obtains utility 𝑈𝑘
𝑁= 𝛾𝑞−𝑝𝑁. Upon returning the product, the customer gets the price

3.1.2. With the RFI service
With the RFI service (𝐼𝑅= 1), the insurance companies generally evaluate the product quality to avoid excessive customer returns led by
misleading quality information, and they are entitled to rescind the contract with high-return-rate retailers, which forces the retailer to post the
true quality as much as possible. Customers know this and can reasonably believe that the information posted by the retailer is more credible with
the RFI service. That is, the RFI service acts as a third-party certificate Hwang et al. (2006) and helps signal the true quality to customers Shao et al.
(2013), Zhang et al. (2021). However, such verification may not always work due to the difficulties in describing the experience-based product’s
performance through text and pictures. In a nutshell, the RFI service is an imperfect signal of product quality.
To capture the RFI’s imperfect signaling effect, we assume that the adoption of RFI reveals the retailer’s true quality with probability 𝜎, where
𝜎∈[0, 1) represents the signal efficiency. The signal efficiency 𝜎is associated with the product category. For example, before using the product,
customers know less about the quality of experience-based products such as apparel and make-up. They rely more on the RFI provider’s quality

3.2. The retailer and insurance companies
Note that there are competitive insurance companies offering homogeneous RFI services, and they perfectly compete with each other due to
low barriers to entry. As revealed later, through perfect competition and selling homogeneous RFI, the insurance companies in equilibrium make
zero expected profit.
With competitive insurance companies, the retailer first decides whether to adopt the RFI service and sets the retail price. If the retailer adopts
the RFI service, the insurance companies independently set the RFI premium. The retailer then chooses the insurance company with the lowest
premium 𝑎. Notably, the retailer sets the retail price before selecting the insurance company. By doing so, the retailer exempts the insurance
companies from the risk of a disadvantageous retail price, which helps reduce the premium. This decision sequence also echoes the practice that
after the retailer uses RFI, the online shopping platform allows insurance companies to adjust the premium based on the retailer’s real-time data 7.
If the retailer adopts the RFI service, it pays the selected insurance company the RFI premium 𝑎for each product sold. When the customer
returns the purchase, the RFI service provider compensates the customer 𝛽𝑓, where 𝛽∈(0, 1). For each returned product, the retailer spends a
handling cost 𝑡.10 The retailer’s profit can be expressed as 𝛱𝑅(𝑝𝑅) = 𝑝𝑅𝑑𝑘
𝑅−𝑡𝑑𝑟
𝑅−𝑎𝑑𝑅if the RFI service is adopted; otherwise, it obtains profit
𝛱𝑁(𝑝𝑁) = 𝑝𝑁𝑑𝑘
𝑁−𝑡𝑑𝑟
𝑁. The expected profit of the RFI service provider can be expressed by 𝜋(𝑎) = 𝑎𝑑𝑅−𝛽𝑓𝑑𝑟
𝑅. The notations and comments are
summarized in Table 1.
We summarize the event sequence as follows:
(1) The retailer decides whether to adopt the RFI service and sets the corresponding retail price.
(2) The competing insurance companies determine the RFI premiums, and the retailer chooses the insurance company.
(3) The customers update their beliefs about the product quality and make purchase decisions, and the retailer pays the selected insurance
company the premium based on the sales volume.
(4) The customers learn the true product quality and decide whether to return their purchases.

𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿.

In the next section, we investigate the optimal pricing strategies of the retailer by considering two cases: without RFI service and with RFI
service. We also explore the RFI premium strategy of the insurance companies under the RFI case. We finally identify the conditions under which
the retailer adopts the RFI service by trading off the expected profits with the RFI service and without the service.
4. Analysis

Conventional wisdom tells that the high-quality retailer should target both the compulsive and strategic customers since all targeted ones
would keep the product. However, a relatively large return freight fee potentially indicates a low expected utility and a shrinking demand of the
strategic customers. In this case, the retailer only accommodates the compulsive customers and sets a low price to expand the volume of compulsive
customers. Moreover, the price is independent of the return freight fee as the expected utility of the compulsive customers is irrelevant to the return
freight fee. As the return freight fee drops, the expected utility and the volume of strategic customers increase; thus, the high-quality retailer can
accommodate both compulsive and strategic customers to expand the market and therefore charge a higher price.
Intriguingly, the low-quality retailer can employ the same price as the high-quality retailer if the return freight fee is large enough (see case
2-(i)). This is because the retailer only accommodates the compulsive customers, who cannot distinguish between the low-quality and high-quality
retailers at the purchase stage. As the return freight fee slightly drops, the volume of compulsive customers declines. In this case, the retailer should
decrease its price to attract the compulsive customers (see case 2-(ii)). When the return freight fee is low, the high retail price becomes the main
incentive for customer returns, and thus the retailer should further lower the price to expand the volume of compulsive customers. However, in
this case, the low price may also attract strategic customers, which is detrimental to the retailer’s profit as these strategic customers will finally

Lemma 2.
The equilibrium profit 𝛱𝑖
𝑁for the retailer with 𝑞= 𝑞𝑖, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿has the following properties:
(1) The high-quality (𝑞= 𝑞𝐻) retailer’s profit 𝛱𝐻
𝑁is non-increasing in the return freight fee 𝑓;
(2) The low-quality (𝑞= 𝑞𝐿) retailer’s profit 𝛱𝐿
𝑁is non-decreasing in the return freight fee 𝑓.
Lemma 2 indicates that the return freight fee has the opposite impact on the profits of the retailers with different quality. The high-quality
retailer tends to accommodate both the compulsive and strategic customers as it bears no customer returns. A large return freight fee inhibits the
strategic customers’ purchases, impeding total demand and harming the retailer’s profit. Contrastively, for the low-quality retailer, a high return
freight fee enlarges the volume of compulsive customers who never return the low-quality product and thus benefits the retailer.
4.2. Case with the RFI service
In this section, we discuss the situation where the retailer adopts the RFI service (𝐼𝑅= 1). Similar to the benchmark case, we first analyze the
customers’ return behavior and categorize them into three groups according to their WTP: 1) the compulsive customers with high WTP 𝛾≥𝑝𝑅−(1−𝛽)𝑓

behavior, we obtain the purchase behavior of each group of customers by exploiting their expected utilities. We relegate the customer’s purchase
behavior to Appendix A.
We then analyze the RFI premium 𝑎of the RFI service provider based on the customer’s return and purchase behaviors. Recall that there are
plenty of insurance companies due to free entry on an e-commerce platform. In addition, these competitive insurance companies sell homogeneous
RFI services, and are perfectly substitutable with each other. Thus, each insurance company manages to set the lowest RFI premium to acquire the
whole market, resulting in an RFI premium that makes zero expected profit (Edgeworth, 1925; Rothschild and Stiglitz, 1978; Azevedo and Gottlieb,
2017), i.e., 𝜋(𝑎) = 𝑎𝑑𝑅−𝛽𝑓𝑑𝑟

Proposition 1-(1) shows that the RFI premium is closely related to the equilibrium return rate. This is in line with the practice that the retailer’s
return rate is the main factor for insurance companies, e.g., Huatai Insurance and PICC, to determine the RFI premium. Specifically, regarding
the high-quality retailer, the insurance companies charge infinitesimally small premiums, since both the compulsive and strategic customers keep
the high-quality product. By contrast, the low-quality retailer may encounter customer returns from the strategic customers (e.g., when 𝑓≤𝐹𝑙as
shown in Table 3). Therefore, the insurance companies potentially charge large premiums for those low-quality retailers with high return rate. This
result differs from the expected-quality related premium indicated in Zhang et al. (2021). The distinction is driven by the information structure of
the insurance company. Specifically, they assume the insurance company lacks the retailer’s quality information; instead, the true quality is known
to the insurance company in our study. Later, we show how the return freight fee and signal efficiency affect the equilibrium return rate as well
as the optimal RFI premium.
Similar to the benchmark case, Proposition 1-(2) shows that the retailer selling high-quality products charges a price to accommodate both the
compulsive and strategic customers if the return freight fee is small, or sets a price to accommodate only the compulsive customers if the return
freight fee is large. As Proposition 1-(3) and Table 3 depict, the retailer selling low-quality products charges a price only to accommodate the

the retailer charges a price to accommodate both strategic and compulsive customers, thus paying a large RFI premium.
A high signal efficiency improves the customer’s utility regarding the high-quality product but reduces the counterpart of the low-quality
product. Hence, along with the increase of signal efficiency, the high-quality retailer prefers to accommodate both the compulsive and strategic
customers and to improve the price, as shown in Fig. 2(a). By contrast, the retailer with low quality should take into account the potential returns
of the strategic customers and be unwilling to accommodate them. It follows that the low-quality retailer charges high prices to focus only on the
compulsive customers when the signal efficiency is high (see Fig. 2(b)).
Corollary 1.
The optimal retail price 𝑝𝑖
𝑅, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿has the following properties:
(1) For the high-quality retailer, the retail price 𝑝𝐻
𝑅is non-increasing in the return freight fee 𝑓, and is increasing in the signal efficiency 𝜎;
(2) For the low-quality retailer, the retail price 𝑝𝐿
𝑅: (i) is decreasing in the return freight fee 𝑓if 𝑓≤𝐹𝑙,
𝑞𝐿
𝑞𝐻≤max{ 2𝛽−1
𝛽
, 0}; (ii) is non-decreasing in
𝑓otherwise;
(3) For the low-quality retailer, the retail price 𝑝𝐿
𝑅: (i) is increasing in the signal efficiency 𝜎if 𝐹𝑙≤𝑓≤(1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
; (ii) is non-increasing in 𝜎otherwise.
As the return freight fee increases, the strategic customers become reluctant to purchase, so the high-quality retailer should either lower the
price to attract the strategic customers, or employ a fixed price to focus only on the compulsive customers. With the high-quality product, the retail
price is positively associated with the signal efficiency because a large signal efficiency improves the customer’s expected utility by signaling the
high quality.
For the low-quality retailer, it is clear that, given the price, the volume of compulsive customers is non-decreasing in the return freight fee,
whereas the volume of strategic customers is decreasing in the return freight fee. In addition, the volume of strategic customers increases in the
quality difference between the low- and high-quality levels (in short, quality dissimilarity), because a significant quality dissimilarity enlarges the
gap between the perceived and true quality, improving the volume of strategic customers. The presence of RFI pushes out this quality dissimilarity
effect due to the quality signaling role, thereby reducing the volume of strategic customers. As analyzed above, when the return freight fee is small,
the low-quality retailer must accommodate a proportion of strategic customers, which harms the retailer’s profit. With narrow quality dissimilarity,
the volume of strategic customers is very small in the presence of RFI. In this case, the retailer tends to decrease its price to expand the volume
of compulsive customers along with the increase of the return freight fee. However, when the volume of the strategic customers or the volume of
the compulsive customers is large, the retailer should increase its price to take advantage of the high WTP of the compulsive ones along with the
increase of the return freight fee.
When quality is low, the intuition tells that the retail price should decrease in the signal efficiency as a high signal efficiency reduces the
customer’s purchase utility and impedes the demand. Intriguingly, the retail price increases in the signal efficiency when the return freight fee
is medium (𝐹𝑙≤𝑓≤(1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
). With medium return freight fees, the volume of compulsive customers is not large; meanwhile, the strategic


========== PAGE 9 ==========
International Journal of Production Economics 256 (2023) 108752
9
X. Li et al.
buyers tend to purchase if the signal efficiency is low. Thus, the retailer charges a low price to prevent the strategic customers from returning
their purchases for full refunds. By contrast, when the signal efficiency is high, the volume of strategic customers is small due to the low purchase
utility. Thus, the retailer does not worry too much about the potential returns from strategic customers, and charges a relatively high price to take
advantage of the high WTP of the compulsive customers.
As the RFI premium is affected by the retailer’s return rate, we then investigate the impacts of the return freight fee and the signal efficiency
on the retailer’s return rate.
Lemma 3.
The equilibrium return rate 𝑑𝑟𝑖
𝑅∕𝑑𝑖
𝑅, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿is non-increasing in the return freight fee 𝑓and the signal efficiency 𝜎.
The high-quality retailer encounters no customer returns, thus the return rate is zero and is independent of 𝑓and 𝜎. For the low-quality retailer,
intuitively, a large return freight fee reduces the return rate. A large signal efficiency impedes the strategic customers’ purchases and subsequent
returns by signaling true quality. Thus, a high signal efficiency reduces the return rate.
Proposition 2.
The RFI premium 𝑎𝐻is independent of the return freight fee 𝑓and the signal efficiency 𝜎. The RFI premium 𝑎𝐿is inversely U-shaped in
the return freight fee 𝑓; 𝑎𝐿is decreasing in the signal efficiency 𝜎.
Proposition 2 indicates that the RFI premium for the low-quality retailer is non-monotonic in the return freight fee. The premium is determined
by the unit compensation fee and the retailer’s return rate. When the return freight fee is small, the retailer sells to both the compulsive and
strategic customers, and the return rate is rather stable. Thus, the RFI premium is mainly determined by the compensation fee, and is increasing in
the return freight fee. When the return freight fee is large, the retailer tends to accommodate only the compulsive customers, and thus the return
rate decreases quickly in the return freight fee. In this case, the RFI premium is decreasing in the return freight fee. Moreover, an efficient quality
signal of RFI significantly decreases the volume of strategic customers and the return rate, thus resulting in a small premium. For example, on
Suning.com, AXA insurance company charges a smaller premium for make-up (0.85 RMB) than food (0.95 RMB) and household appliances (1.00
RMB)9.
Finally, we analyze how the return freight fee and the signal efficiency affect the retailer’s profit under the RFI case.
Proposition 3.
The retailer’s profit 𝛱𝑖
𝑅, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿has the following properties:
(1) The high-quality retailer’s profit 𝛱𝐻
𝑅is increasing in the signal efficiency 𝜎; 𝛱𝐻
𝑅is non-increasing in the return freight fee 𝑓.
(2) The low-quality retailer’s profit 𝛱𝐿
𝑅is decreasing in the signal efficiency 𝜎if 𝑓≥(1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
, is inversely U-shaped in the signal efficiency 𝜎if
𝐹𝑙≤𝑓≤(1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
, and is increasing in the signal efficiency 𝜎if 𝑓≤𝐹𝑙.
(3) The low-quality retailer’s profit 𝛱𝐿
𝑅is U-shaped in the return freight fee 𝑓if 𝑓≤𝐹𝑙,
𝑞𝐿
𝑞𝐻≤max{ 2𝛽−1
𝛽
, 0}; otherwise, 𝛱𝐿
𝑅is non-decreasing in the
return freight fee 𝑓.
The high-quality retailer does not bear customer returns and prefers accommodating both the compulsive and strategic customers. The high
signal efficiency increases the customers’ perceived quality and their purchase motivation. Therefore, the high-quality retailer obtains a larger profit
when the signal efficiency is high than when it is low. However, a large return freight fee always impedes purchases from the strategic customers
and is harmful to the high-quality retailer, consistent with Lemma 2.
For the low-quality retailer, the high signal efficiency drags down the purchase motivation of both the compulsive and strategic customers. The
diminished purchase motivation of the compulsive customers reduces the actual sales volume and thus harms the retailer’s profit (reduced sales
effect). On the other hand, the decreased purchase motivation of the strategic customers reduces potential returns and benefits the low-quality
retailer (reduced return effect). When the return freight fee is large, the reduced sales effect dominates as only the compulsive customers will buy the
product. Thus, the price and profit of the low-quality retailer are decreasing in the signal efficiency. When the return freight fee is small, both the
compulsive and strategic customers will buy the product, and the volume of strategic customers is large, resulting in the dominant reduced return
effect. Thus, the low-quality retailer’s profit is increasing in the signal efficiency. Given a medium return freight fee, if the signal efficiency is low,
both the compulsive and strategic customers may buy the products. In such a case, the reduced return effect dominates, whereby the low-quality
retailer’s profit increases in the signal efficiency. If the signal efficiency is large, the compulsive customers decline significantly. Hence, the reduced
sales effect again dominates, making the low-quality retailer’s profit decrease in the signal efficiency. Overall, the low-quality retailer’s profit is
inversely U-shaped in the signal efficiency when the return freight fee is medium.
Proposition 3-(3) presents a non-linear impact of the return freight fee on the low-quality retailer’s profit, which is distinct from Lemma 2. This
non-linear impact occurs when the retailer has to accommodate the strategic customers (𝑓≤𝐹𝑙). The rationale is as follows. A relatively high
return freight fee increases the volume of compulsive customers (increased sales effect); whereas it increases the unit cost of the RFI provider and
may improve the RFI premium (increased RFI premium effect). The non-linear impact can be attributed to the trade-off between the increased sales
effect and the potential increased RFI premium effect. Note that a slight quality dissimilarity implies a small volume of strategic customers. When
both the quality dissimilarity and the return freight fee are small, the retailer lowers the price to expand the volume of compulsive customers at
the expense of accommodating the strategic customers, resulting in a large RFI premium. Thus, the increased RFI premium effect dominates, and the
retailer’s profit decreases as the return freight fee rises. As the return freight fee increases, the volume of compulsive customers increases, but the
strategic customers are further impeded. In this case, the return rate quickly declines, resulting in a small RFI premium. Thus, the increased sales
effect dominates, and the retailer’s profit increases in the return freight fee. Nevertheless, when the quality dissimilarity becomes large, the retailer
tends to improve the retail price to hinder the strategic customers and to take advantage of the high WTP of compulsive customers. This makes
the increased sales effect dominant, and the retailer’s profit increases as the return freight fee rises. Similarly, when the return freight fee is large,
the strategic customers are greatly hindered but the volume of compulsive customers expands. Hence, the increased sales effect again dominates,
and the retailer’s profit increases in the return freight fee.

4.3. With RFI vs. Without RFI
In this section, we first compare the retailer’s prices under the benchmark case without RFI and the case with RFI. Then, we compare the
retailer’s profits in the two cases to explore the condition under which the retailer adopts the RFI service.
Proposition 4.
The high-quality retailer always charges a larger price with the RFI than without the RFI, i.e., 𝑝𝐻
𝑅≥𝑝𝐻
𝑁. The low-quality retailer charges a
smaller price with the RFI than without the RFI when either 𝑓≥max
{ (1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
, 𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿
4
}
, or 𝑓≤min{𝐹𝑙, 𝑓𝑙}. However, the low-quality retailer charges
a higher price with the RFI than without the RFI when the return freight fee is medium and the signal efficiency is large.
When the product quality is high, the signaling effect of RFI improves the utility of the compulsive and strategic customers who keep the
high-quality product, boosting the sales. Thus, the appearance of RFI endows the high-quality retailer with the capability to pull up the price. This
finding is consistent with the result that the retailer should raise the price by adopting the RFI service in the literature (e.g., Fan and Chen, 2020;
Lin et al., 2020), where they argue that the price markup is driven by the extra RFI premium. Our work gives a new explanation for this result
from the perspective of the signaling effect of RFI.
However, the RFI may compel the low-quality retailer to lower the price, especially when the return freight fee is very large or very small, as
illustrated in Fig. 3. For quite large return freight fees, only the compulsive customers make purchases regardless of the adoption of RFI. However,
the quality signaling effect of RFI dwindles their expected utility of the low-quality product, compelling the retailer to lower the price. Instead,
for quite small return freight fees, the retailer must accommodate the strategic customers regardless of the adoption of RFI, and the volume of
compulsive customers with RFI is smaller than that without RFI. In this case, the retailer tends to decrease its price to expand the volume of
compulsive customers and inhibits purchases from the strategic customers. Intriguingly, when the return freight fee is medium, the retail price
with the RFI may be larger than that without RFI (the specific conditions are shown in Table A.9 in the Appendix A). Specifically, there are two
cases. (1) When the return freight is relatively small, without the RFI, in addition to the compulsive customers, the strategic customers may buy the
products, which is harmful to the low-quality retailer, as aforementioned. With the RFI, the high signal efficiency decreases the strategic customer’s
utility and effectively inhibits their purchases, endowing the low-quality retailer with the ability to increase the sales price to take advantage of
the high WTP of the compulsive customers. (2) As the return freight fee further increases such that only the compulsive customers make purchases
(i.e., when (1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
≤𝑓≤max{ (1−𝜎)(𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿)
4(1−𝛽)
, 𝑞𝐻−𝑞𝐿
4
}), only a relatively low signal efficiency leads to price markups with the RFI. This can be
attributed to the detrimental signaling effect on the compulsive customers.
Furthermore, as the quality dissimilarity narrows, the condition of 𝑝𝐿
𝑅≥𝑝𝐿
𝑁diminishes considerably, as shown in Fig. 3. Note that the volume
of strategic customers shrinks significantly along with the narrowing of quality dissimilarity, especially when the RFI service is not offered. Hence,
the retailer turns to focus on the compulsive customers and charges a higher price without RFI.
We then investigate the retailer’s adoption decision about the RFI service by comparing its profits with the RFI service and without the service.
Proposition 5.
The high-quality retailer always adopts the RFI service. The low-quality retailer adopts the RFI service under some conditions: 1) when
the return freight fee is small and the signal efficiency is large; 2) or when both the return freight fee and the signal efficiency are medium. Otherwise, the
low-quality retailer does not adopt the RFI service.
The RFI adoption decision of the retailer depends on the trade-off between the sales revenue advantage (𝑝𝑅𝑑𝑘
𝑅−𝑝𝑁𝑑𝑘
𝑁) and the RFI cost
(𝑡(𝑑𝑟
𝑅−𝑑𝑟
𝑁) + 𝑎𝑑𝑅), including both the extra return handling cost and the RFI premium. When the product is of high quality, no customers will
return the purchases. Moreover, the RFI service prompts the customer’s purchase utility from the high-quality retailer due to its quality signaling
effect. Thus, the high-quality retailer always adopts the RFI service and raises the price. This finding aligns with the practice that most retailers
on Tmall.com11, where only high-quality retailers on Taobao.com can join, offer the RFI service.
The RFI adoption decision of the low-quality retailer is rather nuanced. As shown in Fig. 4 (the colored area represents the low-quality retailer
prefers adopting the RFI service), this decision is dependent on the signal efficiency and the return freight fee (the specific conditions under which
the low-quality retailer adopts the RFI service are shown in Table A.10 of Appendix A). When the return freight fee is large, only the compulsive
customers buy the products. Even though the RFI service does nothing to the return cost, it reduces customers’ expected utility due to the quality
signaling effect, compelling the retailer to lower the price and ultimately leading to low sales revenue. Hence, no low-quality retailer adopts the
RFI service when the return freight fee is large. This result is consistent with the empirical finding in Chen et al. (2018) and gives a theoretical
explanation that the online retailers selling bulk merchandise (e.g., furniture) are unlikely to adopt the RFI service.

When the return freight fee is medium, the retailer may charge a low price to accommodate both the compulsive and strategic customers, as the
volume of compulsive customers is not large. As aforementioned, due to the signaling effect of RFI, the low-quality retailer can use the RFI service
to effectively prevent strategic customers from purchasing the product. However, the signal efficiency should be medium so that the expected
utility of compulsive customers does not decrease too much. As a result, the low-quality retailer adopts the RFI service when the signal efficiency
is moderate. As is presented in 4, the benefits of using RFI shrink if the quality dissimilarity narrows considerably. Shrinkage mainly occurs when

4(1−𝜎)

. Within this interval, only when using a very large 𝑝𝐿

When the return freight fee is small, the volume of compulsive customers is small, and the low-quality retailer accommodates the strategic
customers regardless of the adoption of RFI. Interestingly, even though the presence of RFI compels the low-quality retailer to lower the price (as
shown in Proposition 4), the low-quality retailer still has incentives to adopt the RFI service when signal efficiency is high. This is because a high
signal efficiency can effectively prevent some strategic customers from purchasing the products, reducing the return rate and the RFI premium. In
addition, the retailer lowers the price to boost the sales of the compulsive ones and to reduce the return motivation of the strategic customer. This
result is in line with the phenomenon that the retailers selling make-up and apparel, for which the signal efficiency is high and the return freight
fee is very small in comparison with the retail price, are more willing to adopt the RFI service.
5. Extension: Monopoly insurance company
Our base model assumes that there are competitive insurance companies. In this section, we consider the case of a monopoly insurance company,
and explore the RFI strategies of the monopolistic insurance company and the retailer. The detailed analysis is relegated to Appendix B.
Note that the retailer first decides the retail price and the adoption of RFI. Upon observing the retailer’s RFI strategy, the insurance company
decides the RFI premium 𝑎𝑖
𝑠, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿. Due to the second mover advantage, the monopolistic insurance company sets the RFI premium as high as
possible. In response to this high premium, the retailer raises the retail price too. As a result, both the demand and the profit of the retailer are
thwarted, and the high- and low-quality retailers have less incentive to adopt the RFI service, which is shown in Fig. 5.
To avoid premium gouging by the insurance company, the retailer might delay its pricing and RFI decisions, if possible. This reverses Steps 1)
and 2) in the game sequence. Under this setting, the insurance company first determines the RFI premium 𝑎𝑖
𝑚, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿. After observing 𝑎𝑖
𝑚, the
retailer decides whether to adopt the RFI service and sets the retail price accordingly.
The postponement of the retailer’s decision allows the monopolistic insurance company to become the leader. Thus the monopolistic insurance
company charges a higher RFI premium than the competitive insurance companies in the base model as indicated in Fig. 6 (note that this RFI
premium is no more than the previous case where the insurance company moves after the retailer). In equilibrium, the high-quality retailer always
buys RFI from the insurance company, but the low-quality retailer has less incentive to use RFI compared to our base model due to the large


========== PAGE 12 ==========
International Journal of Production Economics 256 (2023) 108752

RFI Premium Comparison Between the Case with a Single Insurance Company and the Case with Competitive Insurance Companies. (a) 𝑎𝐻
𝑚vs. 𝑎𝐻. (b) 𝑎𝐿
𝑚vs. 𝑎𝐿.
premium cost. Compared to our base model, this postponement cannot improve the retailer’s profit regardless of the quality level, because the
monopoly insurance company carves up partial supply chain profit, making the retailer worse off.
As indicated above, no matter whether the retailer postpones its decisions, it earns less profit when there is only one insurance company, and
thus has less incentive to use the RFI service. From this perspective, our result explains why online platforms such as Taobao reduce the entry
barriers of the RFI service and encourage more insurance companies to participate.
6. Conclusions
As the MBG policy has been widely adopted, the return freight fee becomes a new barrier for customers to purchase online. To reduce this
adverse effect, online retailers in China buy the RFI service from competitive insurance companies. With the RFI service, insurance companies
evaluate and verify the quality information posted by the online retailer to avoid excessive customer returns, and compensate the customer for the
return freight fee when returns occur. From the view of customers, the RFI service exhibits both the imperfect quality signaling effect and the return
freight fee reduction effect. The RFI’s imperfect signaling effect mitigates the quality risk for customers before purchasing, which is captured by
signal efficiency in our context. We build a game-theoretic model to analyze the impact of these two effects on customer purchase/return behavior,
and the impact on the strategies of the retailer and competitive insurance companies.
Both the RFI’s imperfect signaling effect and return freight fee reduction effect highlight the operational details for insurance companies and
online retailers, where the former has received little attention in the literature. Our results show that the competitive insurance companies set an
infinitesimally small RFI premium for the high-quality retailer. By contrast, they charge a high RFI premium for the low-quality retailer due to large
compensation costs for customer returns. Moreover, when determining the RFI premium, the insurance companies should take return freight fees and
product categories with various signal efficiency into account. This result complements the extant literature in which the RFI premium is assumed
to be exogenous. In particular, insurance companies should assign a small premium for the category with high signal efficiency, e.g., make-up. In
addition, as the return freight fee increases, the insurance company should first raise the premium and then strategically decrease it.
Regarding the RFI adoption decision for the retailer, our result shows that the high-quality retailer is always willing to adopt the RFI service.
One might think that the low-quality retailer does not employ the RFI service due to the quality signaling effect. Interestingly, the low-quality
retailer adopts the RFI service when both the signal efficiency and the return freight fee are medium, or when the signal efficiency is high and the
return freight fee is small. This is because the quality signaling role of RFI can effectively prevent the detrimental purchase of strategic customers
who finally return the product after they learn the true product quality. However, the low-quality retailer does not use RFI when the return freight
fee is large. Thus, the low-quality retailer selling bulk merchandise should be careful in adopting RFI; but the retailer selling make-up and clothing
should offer RFI to reap higher profit.
As for the retail pricing strategy with the RFI service, our results show that the high-quality retailer always employs a price markup with the
adoption of RFI. In particular, the price of the high-quality retailer increases in signal efficiency. However, the low-quality retailer may not enjoy
a price markup by using RFI, especially when the return freight fee is extremely small. In contrast to the high-quality case, the impact of signal
efficiency on the price of the low-quality retailer is not monotonic, depending on the return freight fee. Thus, the low-quality retailer cannot simply
increase the price with the adoption of RFI. Instead, it should take product category into consideration and carefully trade off the sales volume
and the detrimental product returns when determining the retail price.
Finally, we consider how the findings differ if there is a monopoly insurance company. The monopoly insurance company has more power to
set a high RFI premium and makes positive returns. As a result, the retailer earns less profit, and thus has less incentive to use RFI. Our result
explains why online platforms such as Taobao encourage more insurance companies to participate.
Future research may pay more attention to customers’ concerns about horizontal quality (i.e., the risk of product mismatch), and the joint
effects of RFI with other information disclosure strategies. Furthermore, the effect of a more complex market structure (i.e., competitive market)
is of interest. In practice, insurance companies are closely collaborating with shopping platforms, and another important issue is to investigate the
coordination of RFI strategies between the online shopping platform and insurance companies, e.g., the contract design problem.
Data availability
No data was used for the research described in the article.

In summary, we conclude that the low-quality retailer’s profit 𝛱𝐿

□
Proof of Proposition 1.
Under the RFI case, we start with the customer’s return behavior, similar to the benchmark case. Through analyzing
customers’ purchase and return behaviors, we formalize the demand and customer returns. Then we solve the insurance companies’ and retailer’s
pricing strategies regarding the customer’s best reactions.
By comparing 𝑈𝑘

Proof of Proposition 5. We analyze the RFI adoption strategy of the retailer with quality 𝑞𝐻and 𝑞𝐿respectively. As for the retailer with quality
𝑞𝑖, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿, specifically, it would like to adopt the RFI strategy iff 𝛱𝑖

𝑁. Therefore, in the sequel, we aim to find the conditions by comparing

𝑅and 𝛱𝑖
𝑁.
Case I: 𝑞= 𝑞𝐻
First, we compare 𝛱𝐻
𝑅and 𝛱𝐻
𝑁to find when the retailer with 𝑞= 𝑞𝐻prefers the RFI service, i.e., 𝛱𝐻
𝑅≥𝛱𝐻
𝑁. Similar to the proof of 𝑝𝑅𝐻≥𝑝𝑁𝐻,
we first show two algebra facts. Given these facts, we argue that 𝛱𝐻
𝑅≥𝛱𝐻
𝑁always holds regarding the thresholds of the optimal pricing strategies
with/without RFI.
Firstly, we have the following two algebra facts.

≥𝑞𝐻+𝑞𝐿

Proof of Monopoly Insurance Company with Reversed Sequence of. (1) and (2).
With the reversed sequence of (1) and (2), the insurance company first decides the RFI premium 𝑎. Then, the retailer decides whether to adopt
the RFI service and sets the price.
Therefore, given the customer’s return and purchase behaviors in Proposition 1, we next explore the pricing and RFI strategies of the retailer
of 𝑞𝑖, 𝑖= 𝐻, 𝐿respectively. Under this setting, the retailer solves 𝑝𝑅by eliminating the constraint 𝑎= 𝛽𝑓in Optimization Programs (B.2) to (B.4).

the RFI premium 𝑎, and solves the following optimization program.

Even though the RFI premiums are cumbersome to analyze due to the complicated bounds as is shown in Table B.16, we can shape the following
intuitions. For the high-quality retailer, 𝑑𝑟
𝑅= 0, which implies zero compensation cost for the insurance company. Hence, the insurance company
always chooses a small positive 𝑎to gain positive profit and ensure the participation of the retailer. Consequently, in equilibrium, the high-quality
retailer always uses RFI but earns less profit compared to our base model.
